77.0

Performance of individual experts
:
Our approach focuses on training diverse experts, where the
CLS
expert classifier is able to perform well on
Head
(majority) classes, while the
DIST
expert classifier is able to perform well on the
Tail
(minority) classes. By averaging the output of the individual classifiers, we are able to exploit the benefit of both.

In this portion, we discuss the individual performance of the
CLS
and
DIST
expert classifiers of our proposed DeiT-LT method on CIFAR-10 LT, CIFAR-100 LT, ImageNet-LT, and iNaturalist-2018. As can be seen in Table
E.1
and Table
E.2
, the
CLS
and
DIST
classifiers give a contrasting performance on the head and tail classes, supporting our claim of expert classifiers. For CIFAR-10 LT (
\rho
= 100), the
CLS
expert classifier is able to report an accuracy of 96.5% on images of the head classes, whereas the
DIST
expert classifier settles with 72.8% on the same set of classes. On the other hand, the
DIST
expert classifier reports 93.0% accuracy on the tail classes, which is almost 33% more than that of the
CLS
expert classifier. Like CIFAR-10 LT, the
CLS
expert classifier performs better on the head classes of CIFAR-100 LT (
\rho
= 100) than the
DIST
, whereas the
DIST
expert classifier reports much higher accuracy on the tail classes. The
CLS
classifier achieves an accuracy of 73.7% on the head classes, and the
DIST
expert classifier secures 43.1% accuracy on the tail classes.
We notice that by averaging the output of the classifiers, we are able to report good performance in both the majority and the minority classes. CIFAR-10 LT reaches an overall accuracy of 87.3%, with 93.8% on head classes and 85.7% on tail classes. Similarly, with 72.8% on the head and 31.0% on the tail, DeiT-LT is able to secure an overall 54.8% on CIFAR-100 LT. The results demonstrate that there is a parallel trend in the performance of experts for both CIFAR-10 LT and CIFAR-100 LT when
\rho
is set to 50.

A similar trend is seen for large-scale ImageNet-LT and iNaturalist-2018 in Table
E.2
. For ImageNet-LT, the
CLS
expert classifier reports 68.3% accuracy on the head classes, which is approximately 11% more reported by the
DIST
expert classifier. At the same time, we observe that the
DIST
expert classifier is able to get an accuracy of 46.6% on the tail, which is significantly higher than the 13.5% of the
CLS
expert classifier. For iNaturalist-2018 as well, the
CLS
expert classifier achieves a high accuracy of 73.8% on the head classes, and the
DIST
expert classifier reaches 77.0% on the tail classes. After averaging the outputs of the two classifiers, DeiT-LT reports an overall accuracy of 59.1% for ImageNet-LT and 75.1% for iNaturalist-2018, which would not have been possible by training a standard Vision Transformer (ViT) with a single classifier.

E
Comparison with CLIP based methods

Recently, some approaches such as VL-LTR
[
308
]
and PEL
[
291
]
have adopted a pre-trained CLIP backbone to address long-tailed recognition challenges. As indicated originally, and also reinforced by
[
353
]
, CLIP is trained on large-scale balanced dataset (400 M Image-Text pair). As there is a lot of
overlapping concepts between balanced CLIP data and long-tailed datasets (ImageNet-LT and iNat-18)
, the performance of the CLIP fine-tuned methods
does not indicate meaningful progress on long-tail learning tasks
, as CLIP has already seen tail concepts in abundance. Due to this
unfairness
in training datasets used, we refrain from comparing the CLIP fine-tuned models (i.e., VL-LTR, PEL etc.) with DeiT-LT models trained from scratch.

E
Visualization of Attention

To demonstrate the effect of distillation in DeiT-LT, we visualize the attention of baseline methods on ImageNet-LT without distillation (ViT and DeiT-III) and compare it with DeiT-LT. As DeiT-LT contains both the
DIST
token and the
CLS
token, for visualization we average the attention across both. We use the Attention Rollout
[
303
]
method for visualization. Fig.
E.1
shows the result of attention for different methods. It can be clearly observed that DeiT-LT is able to localize attention at the correct position of objects, across almost all cases. We find that DeiT-III attention maps are better in comparison to ViT, but it also often gets confused (eg. Bell Pepper, Sea Snake etc.) compared to DeiT-LT.

:

[

]
and DeiT-LT
(ours)
on the ImageNet-LT dataset, computed using the method of
Attention Rollout

[
3
]
.

E
Statistical Significance of Experiments

In this section, we present the results of our experiments on CIFAR-10 LT and CIFAR-100 LT (
\rho
= 100, 50)(as in Table
E.1
), with three different random seeds. In Table
E.1
, we report the average performance of the expert classifiers along with the standard error for each. The low error demonstrates that the DeiT-LT training procedure is stable and quite robust across random seeds.

E
Details on Local Connectivity Analysis

We compute the mean attention distance for samples of tail classes (i.e. 7,8,9 class for CIFAR-10) using the method proposed by
Raghu et al. 2021
. For each head present in self-attention blocks, we calculate the distance of the patches it attends to. More specifically, we weigh the distance in the pixel space with the attention value and then average it. This is averaged for all the images present in the tail classes. We utilize the code provided here as our reference
7
7

7

https://github.com/sayakpaul/probing-vits
. We show in Fig.
6.4(b)

that for early blocks (1 and 2) of ViT, the proposed DeiT-LT method contains local features.
As we go from ViT to distilled DeiT to proposed DeiT-LT, we find that features become more local, which explains the generalizability of DeiT-LT for tail classes. To further confirm our observations, we also provide local connectivity plots for the tail classes of the CIFAR-100 dataset (Fig.
E.1
). We observe that DeiT-LT produces highly local features. Further, we find that the DeiT baseline (Table
6.2
), which is inferior to ViT for CIFAR-100, shows the presence of global features. Hence, the local connectivity correlates well with generalization on tail classes. The correlation of locality of features to generalization has also been observed by
[
252
]
, who find that using the ImageNet-21k dataset for pre-training leads to more local and generalizable features in comparison to networks pre-trained on ImageNet-1k.

:

E
Distilling low-rank features

In our proposed method, as the
DIST
token serves as the expert on tail classes, it is important to ensure that it learns generalizable features for minority classes that are less prone to overfitting. As stated in
[
12
]
, training a network with SAM optimizer leads to low-rank features. In this subsection, we investigate the feature rank of the
DIST
token that is distilled via a SAM-based teacher.

Calculating Feature Rank.
Consider two sets of images
\mathcal{X}_{all},\mathcal{X}_{min}\subset\mathcal{X}
, where
\mathcal{X}_{all},\mathcal{X}_{min}
refer to the set of images from all the classes and minority (tail) classes, respectively, with

\mathcal{X}
being the set of all images. We construct feature matrices
F_{n_{h},d}^{all}
and
F_{n_{t},d}^{min}
, where
n_{h}
and
n_{t}
are the number of images in
\mathcal{X}_{all}
and
\mathcal{X}_{min}
respectively, and
d
is the dimension of the feature representation from
DIST
token.

Upon centering the columns of
F_{n_{h},d}^{all}
, we decompose the feature matrix as
U,S,V^{T}=\mathsf{SVD}(F_{n_{h},d}^{all})
, and project
F_{n_{t},d}^{min}
using the right singular vectors
V
as

F_{proj}^{min}(k)=F_{n_{t},d}^{min}*V_{k}

where
V_{k}
contains the top
k
singular vectors (principal componenets). We calculate our rank as the least
k
that satisfies

\frac{{||F_{n_{t},d}^{min}-F_{recon}^{min}(k)||}^{2}}{{||F_{n_{t},d}^{min}||}^{2}}\leq 0.01

where
F_{recon}^{min}(k)
is an approximate reconstructed feature matrix given by
F_{recon}^{min}(k)=F_{proj}^{min}(k)*{V_{k}}^{T}
.

As shown in Fig.
2(b)
, we find that the
DIST
token trained with a SAM-based teacher reports a lower rank. As we are able to use the same principal components to represent both the majority and minority classes’ feature representation, it signifies that the
DIST
token learns generalizable characteristics relevant across different categories of images in an imbalanced dataset. By learning semantic similar features, our training of
DIST
token ensures good representation learning for minority classes by leveraging the discriminative features learned from majority classes.

On the other hand, we observe that
CLS
token learns high-rank feature representations (Fig.
2(a)
), signifying that it captures intricately detailed information. Our DeiT-LT, thus, captures a wide range of information by using the predictions made using both fine-grained details from
CLS
token and generalizable features from
DIST
token.

E.1
Convergence Analysis with SAM Teachers

We find that models distilled from the teachers trained using SAM
[
80
]
converge faster than the usual CNN teachers. We provide the analysis for the Deit-LT(PaCo+SAM) and DeiT-LT(PaCo) on the ImageNet-LT and CIFAR-100 datasets in Fig.
E.3
. We observe that models with SAM, coverage much faster, particularly for the ImageNet-LT dataset, demonstrating the increased convergence speed for the distillation. This can be attributed to the fact that low-rank models are simpler in structure and are much easier to distill to the transformer.

E
Computation Requirement

For training our proposed DeiT-LT method on CIFAR-10 LT and CIFAR-100 LT, we use two NVIDIA RTX 3090 GPU cards with 24 GiB memory each, with both datasets requiring about 15 hours to train to train the ViT student. We train the DeiT-LT student network on four NVIDIA RTX A5000 GPU cards for the large-scale ImageNet-LT dataset and on four NVIDIA A100 GPU cards for the iNaturalist-2018 dataset, in 61 and 63 hours, respectively.

F. Cost-Sensitive Self-Training for Optimizing

Non-Decomposable Metrics (Chapter-7)

F
Limitations and Negative Societal Impacts

F.1
Limitations of our Work

At this point we only consider optimizing objectives through
CSST
which can be written as a linear combination of entries of confusion matrix. Although there are important metrics like Recall, Coverage etc. which can be expressed as a linear form of confusion matrix. However, there do exist important metrics like Intersection over Union (IoU), Q-Mean etc. which we don’t consider in the current work.
We leave this as an open direction for further work.

Also in this work we considered datasets where unlabeled data distribution doesn’t significantly differ from the labeled data distribution, developing robust methods which can also take into account the distribution shift between unlabeled and labeled is an interesting direction for future work.

F.2
Negative Societal Impact

Our work has application in fairness domain
[
60
]
, where it can be used to improve performance of minority sub-groups present in data. These fairness objectives can be practically enforced on neural networks through the proposed
CSST
framework.
However these same algorithms can be tweaked to artifically induce bias in decision making of trained neural networks, for example by ignoring performance of models on certain subgroups. Hence, we suggest deployment of these models after through testing on all sub groups of data.

F
Connection between Minimization of Weighted Consistency Regularizer Loss (Eq. (
7.8
)) and
Theoretical Weighted Consistency (
R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)
in Sec.
7.3.4
)

The theoretical weighted consistency regularization can be approximated under strong augmentation
\mathcal{A}
as
R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)\approx\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\bm{1}(F(\mathcal{A}(x))\neq F(x))\right]
. Noting that
\bm{1}(F(\mathcal{A}(x))\neq F(x))\leq\bm{1}(F(\mathcal{A}(x))\neq j)+\bm{1}(F(x)\neq j)
for any class label
j
,
this value of
R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)
is bounded as follows:

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\bm{1}(F(\mathcal{A}(x))\neq F(x))\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\bm{1}(F(\mathcal{A}(x))\neq j))\right]+\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\bm{1}(F(x)\neq j)\right].

Assuming
F(x)
to be composed of a classifier made of neural network model output
p_{m}(x)
. For consistency regularization, we choose samples which have a low second term by thresholding on confidence for which we obtain the pseudo-label
F(x)=\hat{p}_{m}(x)
. In that case the minimization of (the upper bound of)
R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)
is approximately equivalent to cost-sensitive learning (CSL):

\min_{F}\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\bm{1}(F(\mathcal{A}(x))\neq j))\right].

The above objective can be equivalently expressed in form of gain matrix
\mathbf{G}
where
G_{i,j}=w_{i,j}/(\pi_{i})
. Hence, the above minimization can be effectively done for different gain matrices based on corresponding non-decomposable metric
7.2.1
. Following
Narasimhan and Menon 2021
, we can effectively minimize the
R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)
using the calibrated CSL loss function by plugging in the pseudo label
\hat{p}_{m}(x)
in place of ground truth label (
\mathbf{y}
). The final weighted regularization function can be seen as:

\ell^{hyb}(\hat{p}_{m}(x),p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x));\mathbf{G})

(14)

The above regularization loss is same as the weighted consistency regularization loss in Eq. (
7.8
), which leads to minimization of
\mathcal{D}_{KL}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{T}}\hat{p}_{m}(x))||p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x)))\;\forall x\in\mathcal{X}
as seen in Prop.
7.1
. Hence, the minimization of theoretical consistency regularization term is achieved by minimizing the weighted consistency loss.

F
Additional Examples and Proof of Theorem
7.4

In this section, we provide some examples for assumptions introduced in Sec.
7.3

and a proof of Theorem
7.4
.
We provide proof of these examples in Sec.
F.4
.

F.1
Examples for Theoretical Assumptions

The following example (Example
F.3
) shows that the
c
-expansion (Definition
7.1
) property is satisfied for mixtures of Gaussians and mixtures of manifolds.

Example F.3
.

By
[
342
, Examples 3.4, 3.5]
,
the
c
-expansion property is satisfied for mixtures of
isotropic Gaussian distributions and mixtures of manifolds.
More precisely, in the case of mixtures of isotropic Gaussian distributions,
i.e., if
Q
is given as mixtures of
\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i},\frac{1}{d}I_{d\times d})

for
i=1,\dots,n
with some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}

and
\tau_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and
\mathcal{B}(x)
is an
\ell_{2}
-ball with radius
r

then by
[
28
, (13)]
and
[
342
, Section B.2]
,

Q
satisfies the
c
-expansion property
with

c(p)=R_{h}(p)/p
for
p>0

and
h=2r\sqrt{d}

(c.f.,
[
342
, section B.2]
).
Here
R_{h}(p)=\Phi(\Phi^{-1}(p)+h)

and
\Phi
is the cumulative distribution function of the
standard normal distribution on
\mathbb{R}
.

In Sec.
7.3.4
, we required the assumption that
\gamma>3
(Assumption
7.3
)
and remarked that it roughly requires
\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})
is “small”.
The following example provides explicit conditions for
\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})
that satisfy the assumption using a toy example.

Example F.4
.

Using a toy example provided in Example
F.3
,
we provide conditions that satisfy the assumption
\gamma>3
approximately.
To explain the assumption
\gamma>3
, we assume that

\mathcal{P}_{w}
is given as a mixture of isotropic Gaussians and
\mathcal{B}(x)
is
\ell_{2}
-ball with radius
r

as in Example
F.3
.
Furthermore, we assume that

|w|_{1}=1
and
\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})
is sufficiently small compared to
\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})
.
Then,
p_{w}=\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})+\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})\approx\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})
.
Using this approximation,
since
\mathcal{P}_{w}
satisfies the
c
-expansion property with
c(p)=R_{2r\sqrt{d}}(p)/p
,
if
r=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}}
then, the condition
\gamma>3

is satisfied when
\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})<0.17
.
If
r=\frac{3}{2\sqrt{d}}
then, the condition
\gamma>3

is satisfied when
\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})<0.33
.

In Assumption
7.1
, we assumed that both of
\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})
and
R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F^{*})
are small.
The following example suggests the validity of this assumption.

Example F.5
.

In this example,
we assume
w
is a diagonal matrix
\mathrm{diag}(w_{1},\dots,w_{K})
.
For simplicity, we normalize
w
so that
\sum_{i\in[K]}w_{i}=1
.
As in
[
342
, Example 3.4]
, we assume
that
P_{i}
is given as isotropic Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i},\frac{1}{d}I_{d\times d})

with
\tau_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
i=1,\dots,K
and
\mathcal{B}(x)
is an
\ell^{2}
-ball with radius
\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}}
.
Furthermore,
we assume
\inf_{1\leq i<j\leq K}\|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}\|_{2}\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{\log d}}{\sqrt{d}}

and
\sup_{i,j\in[K]}\frac{w_{i}}{w_{j}}=o(d)
, where the latter assumption is valid for high dimensional
datasets (e.g., image datasets).
Then it can be proved that there exists a classifier
F

such that
R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)=O(\frac{1}{d^{c}})
and
\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F)=O(\frac{1}{d^{c}})
,
where
c>0
is a constant (we can take
F
as the Bayes-optimal classifier for
\mathrm{Err}_{w}
).
Thus, this suggests that Assumption
7.1
is valid for
datasets with high dimensional instances.

The statement of Example
F.4
follows from numerical computation of
R_{2r\sqrt{d}}(p)/p
. We provide proofs of Examples
F.3
and
F.5
in Sec.
F.4
.

F.2
Proof of Theorem
7.4
Assuming a Lemma

Theorem
7.4
can be deduced from the following lemma (by taking
H=F^{*}
and

\mathcal{L}_{Q,H}(\widehat{F})\leq\mathcal{L}_{Q,H}(F^{*})
),
which provides a similar result to
[
342
, Lemma A.8]
.

Lemma F.6
.

Let
H
be a classifier and

Q
a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}
satisfying
c
-expansion property.
We put
\gamma_{H}=c(Q(\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq H(x)\}))
.
For a classifier
F
, we define
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)
by

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)=\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F(x)=F(x^{\prime})\quad\forall x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(x)\}.

For a classifier
F
, we define
\mathcal{L}_{Q,H}(F)
by

\frac{\gamma_{H}+1}{\gamma_{H}-1}Q(\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F(x)\neq F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\})\\
+\frac{2\gamma_{H}}{\gamma_{H}-1}Q(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F))+\frac{2\gamma_{H}}{\gamma_{H}-1}Q(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(H))-Q(\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq H(x)\}),

where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F)
denotes the complement of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)
.
Then, we have
Q(\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F(x)\neq H(x)\})\leq\mathcal{L}_{Q,H}(F)

for any classifier
F
.

In this subsection, we provide a proof of Theorem
7.4

assuming Lemma
F.6
.
We provide a proof of the lemma in the next subsection.
For a classifier
F
, we define
\mathcal{M}(F)
as
\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F(x)\neq F^{*}(x)\}

and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)
as
\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F(x)\neq F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\}
.
We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{w}(F)
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{w}(F)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{w}(F)+\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F^{*})-\mathcal{P}_{w}(\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq F^{*}(x)\}).

We note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{w}(F)-\mathcal{L}_{w}(F)
does not depend on
F
.

Proof F.7

(Proof of Theorem
7.4
)
.

We let
Q=\mathcal{P}_{w}
and
H=F^{*}
in Lemma
F.6

and denote
\gamma_{H}
in the lemma by
\gamma^{\prime}
.
Since
w_{ij}\geq 0
and
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\mathds{1}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq F^{*}(x))\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\mathds{1}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq j)\right]+\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\mathds{1}(F^{*}(x)\neq j)\right]

for any
i,j
, we have the following:

\displaystyle|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\mathds{1}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq F^{*}(x))\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\mathds{1}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq j)\right]+\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\mathds{1}(F^{*}(x)\neq j)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})+\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*}).

Here, the first equation follows from the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{w}
.
Thus, we obtain
\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}))\leq p_{w}
.
By definition of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}

(
\gamma=c(p_{w})
and
\gamma^{\prime}=c\left(\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}))\right)
)
and the assumption that
c
is non-increasing, we have the following:

\gamma\leq\gamma^{\prime}.

(15)

We note that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\mathds{1}(F(x)\neq j)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\mathds{1}(F(x)\neq F^{*}(x))\right]+\sum_{i,j\in[K]}w_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{i}}\left[\mathds{1}(F^{*}(x)\neq j)\right]

\displaystyle=|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{M}(F))+\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*}).

(16)

Here the first inequality holds since

\mathds{1}(F(x)\neq j)\leq\mathds{1}(F(x)\neq F^{*}(x))+\mathds{1}(F^{*}(x)\neq j)

for any
x
and
j
.
By (
16
) and Lemma
F.6
, the error is upper bounded as follows:

\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F)\leq\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})+\frac{\gamma^{\prime}+1}{\gamma^{\prime}-1}|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F))\\
+\frac{2\gamma^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}-1}|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F))+\frac{2\gamma^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}-1}|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F^{*}))-|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})).

By (
15
), we obtain

\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F)\leq\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})+\frac{\gamma+1}{\gamma-1}|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F))\\
+\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F))+\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F^{*}))-|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})).

(17)

By definition of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
and letting
F=\widehat{F}
, we have the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{w}(\widehat{F})\leq\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})+\mathcal{L}_{w}(\widehat{F})+\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F^{*})-|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq F^{*}(x)\})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})+\mathcal{L}_{w}(F^{*})+\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F^{*})-|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq F^{*}(x)\})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}|w|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}))+\frac{4\gamma}{\gamma-1}R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})+\frac{2}{\gamma-1}(\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})+\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*}))+\frac{4\gamma}{\gamma-1}R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\gamma-1}\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})+\frac{\gamma+1}{\gamma-1}\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})+\frac{4\gamma}{\gamma-1}R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F^{*}).

Here, the second inequality holds since
\widehat{F}
is a minimizer of
\mathcal{L}_{w}
,
the third inequality follows from

\mathds{1}(F^{*}(x)\neq F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x))\leq\mathds{1}(F^{*}(x)\neq j)+\mathds{1}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq j)
for any
j
.
Thus, we have the assertion of the theorem.

F.3
Proof of Lemma
F.6

We decompose
\mathcal{M}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H)
into the following three sets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H):F(x)=F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x),\text{ and }F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq H(x)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H):F(x)\neq F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x),F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq H(x),\text{ and }F(x)\neq H(x)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H):F(x)\neq F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\text{ and }F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)=H(x)\}.

Lemma F.8
.

Let
S=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H)

and
V=\mathcal{M}(F)\cap\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S
.
Then, we have
\mathcal{N}(V)\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}(F)\cap S=\emptyset

and
\mathcal{N}(V)\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)
.
Here
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)
is defined as
\{x\in\mathcal{X}:F(x)\neq F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\}
.

Proof F.9
.

We take any element
x
in
\mathcal{N}(V)\cap S
.
Since
x\in\mathcal{N}\left(V\right)
and definition of neighborhoods,
there exists
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap\mathcal{M}(F)\cap S
such that

\mathcal{B}(x)\cap\mathcal{B}(x^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
.
Since
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)
,

F
takes the same values on
\mathcal{B}(x)
and
\mathcal{B}(x^{\prime})
.
By
\mathcal{B}(x)\cap\mathcal{B}(x^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
,
F
takes the same value on
\mathcal{B}(x)\cup\mathcal{B}(x^{\prime})
.
It follows that
F(x)=F(x^{\prime})
.
Since we have
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H)
, similarly, we see that
H(x)=H(x^{\prime})
.
By
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(F)
, we have
F(x)=F(x^{\prime})\neq H(x^{\prime})=H(x)
.
Therefore, we have
x\in\mathcal{M}(F)
.
Thus, we have proved that

x\in\mathcal{N}(V)\cap S
implies
x\in\mathcal{M}(F)
, i.e.,

\mathcal{N}(V)\cap S\subseteq\mathcal{M}(F)
.
Therefore, we have
\mathcal{N}(V)\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}(F)\cap S=\emptyset
.
This completes the proof of the first statement.
Next, we assume

x\in\mathcal{N}(V)\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S
.
Then, we have
F(x)\neq H(x)
and
F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)=H(x)
.
Therefore, we obtain
F(x)\neq F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)
.
This completes the proof.

Lemma F.10
.

Suppose that assumptions of Lemma
F.6
hold.
We define
q
as follows:

q=\frac{Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F)\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(H))}{\gamma_{H}-1}.

(18)

Then, we have
Q(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H)\cap\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap\mathcal{M}(F))\leq q
.
In particular, noting that

\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H)\cap\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap\mathcal{M}(F)
,
we have
Q(\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{2})\leq q
.

Proof F.11
.

We let
S=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H)
and
V=\mathcal{M}(F)\cap\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S
as before.
Then by Lemma
F.8
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(V)\cap V^{c}\cap S

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{N}(V)\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}(F)\cap S\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{N}(V)\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\emptyset\cup\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(V)\cap V^{c}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(V)\cap V^{c}\cap(S\cup S^{c})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{N}(V)\cap V^{c}\cap S\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{N}(V)\cap V^{c}\cap S^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)\cup S^{c}.

Thus, by the
c
-expansion property, we have

\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)\cup S^{c})

\displaystyle\geq Q(\mathcal{N}(V)\cap V^{c})

\displaystyle\geq Q(\mathcal{N}(V))-Q(V)

\displaystyle\geq\left(c(Q(V))-1\right)Q(V).

Since
V\subseteq\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})
,
c
is non-increasing, and
\gamma_{H}>1
,
we have
Q(V)\leq Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)\cup S^{c})/(\gamma_{H}-1)\leq q
.
This completes the proof.

The following lemma provides an upper bound of
Q(\mathcal{N}_{3})
.

Lemma F.12
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Lemma
F.6
hold.
We have

Q(\mathcal{N}_{3})\leq q+Q(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F)\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(H))+Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F))-Q(\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})),

where
q
is defined by (
18
).

Proof F.13
.

We let
S=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H)
.
First, we prove

\mathcal{N}_{3}\sqcup\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\cap S\right)=\mathcal{N}_{1}\sqcup\left(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S\right).

(19)

Here, for sets
A,B
, we denote union
A\cup B
by
A\sqcup B
if the union is disjoint.
By definition, we have
\mathcal{N}_{1}=S\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\cap\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})

and
\mathcal{N}_{3}=S\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{3}\cup\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\cap S\right)

\displaystyle=\left(S\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\cap S\right)

\displaystyle=S\cap\left\{\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F)\cap\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\right)\cup\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\right\}

\displaystyle=S\cap\left(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cup\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\right).

Similarly, we have the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\left(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S\right)

\displaystyle=\left(S\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\cap\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S\right)

\displaystyle=S\cap\left(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cup\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\right).

Since disjointness is obvious by definition, we obtain (
19
).
Next, we note that the following holds:

\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\cap S)

\displaystyle=Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F))-Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\cap S^{c})

\displaystyle\geq Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F))-Q(S^{c}).

(20)

By (
19
), we obtain the following:

\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{N}_{3})

\displaystyle=Q(\mathcal{N}_{1})+Q(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S)-Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F)\cap S)

\displaystyle\leq Q(\mathcal{N}_{1})+Q(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}))-Q(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})\cap S)

\displaystyle\leq Q(\mathcal{N}_{1})+Q(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}))-Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F))+Q(S^{c})

\displaystyle\leq q+Q(\mathcal{M}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}))-Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}^{c}(F))+Q(S^{c})

\displaystyle=q-Q(\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}))+Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F))+Q(S^{c}).

Here the second inequality follows from (
20
)
and the third inequality follows from Lemma
F.10
.
This completes the proof.

Now, we can prove Lemma
F.6
as follows.

Proof F.14

(Proof of Lemma
F.6
)
.

\displaystyle Q(\mathcal{M}(F))

\displaystyle=Q(\mathcal{M}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(H))+Q\left(\mathcal{M}(F)\cap\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F)\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(H)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq Q(\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{2})+Q(\mathcal{N}_{3})+Q(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F)\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(H))

\displaystyle\leq 2q+2Q(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F)\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(H))+Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F))-Q(\mathcal{M}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})).

Here, the last inequality follows from Lemmas
F.10
and
F.12
.

Since
q
satisfies
q\leq\frac{Q(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pl}}(F))+Q(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F)+Q(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(H)))}{\gamma_{H}-1}

by (
18
), we have our assertion.

F.4
Miscellaneous Proofs for Examples

Proof F.15

(Proof of Example
F.3
)
.

In Example
F.3
, we stated that

p\mapsto R_{h}(p)/p
is non-increasing.
This follows from the concavity of
R_{h}

and
\lim_{p\rightarrow+0}R_{h}(p)=0
.
In fact, we can prove the concavity of
R_{h}

by
\frac{d^{2}R_{h}}{dp^{2}}(p)=-h\exp\left(\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}-h\xi-\frac{1}{2}h^{2}\right)\leq 0
,
where
\xi=\Phi^{-1}(p)
.

Proof F.16

(Proof of Example
F.5
)
.

For each
i,j\in[K]
,
w_{i}P_{i}(x)\geq w_{j}P_{j}(x)
is equivalent to

(x-\tau_{i})\cdot v_{ji}\leq\frac{\|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}\|}{2}+\frac{2(\log w_{i}-\log w_{j})}{d\|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}\|}
,
where
v_{ji}=\frac{\tau_{j}-\tau_{i}}{\|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}\|}
.
Thus, for each
i\in[K]
,
we have
\bigcap_{j\in[K]\setminus\{i\}}X_{ij}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(F_{\mathrm{opt}})
.
Here
X_{ij}
is defined as
\{x\in\mathcal{X}:(x-\tau_{i})\cdot v_{ji}\leq\frac{\|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}\|}{2}+\frac{2(\log w_{i}-\log w_{j})}{d\|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}\|}-\frac{r}{2}\}
.
For any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|w\|_{2}=\sqrt{d}
and
a>0
,
we have
P_{i}(\{x\in\mathcal{X}:(x-\tau_{i})\cdot w>a\})=1-\Phi(a)\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp(-a^{2}/2)

(c.f.,
[
54
]
).
Thus,
P_{i}(X_{ij}^{c})\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp(-da_{ij}^{2}/2)
, where

a_{ij}=\frac{\|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}\|}{2}+\frac{2(\log w_{i}-\log w_{j})}{d\|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}\|}-\frac{r}{2}
.
By assumptions, we have
a_{ij}\sqrt{d}\gtrsim\sqrt{\log d}
.
Therefore,
P_{i}(X_{ij}^{c})=O(\frac{1}{poly(d)})
.
It follows that

P_{i}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(F_{\mathrm{opt}}))\leq\sum_{j\in[K]\setminus\{i\}}P_{i}(X_{ij}^{c})=O(\frac{1}{poly(d)})
.
Thus, we have
R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F_{\mathrm{opt}})=O(\frac{1}{poly(d)})
.
By the same way, we can prove that
\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{opt}})=O(\frac{1}{poly(d)})
.

F
All-Layer Margin Generalization Bounds

Following
[
342
,
341
]
, we introduce
all layer margin of neural networks and provide generalization bounds of
CSST
. In this section, we assume that classifier
F(x)
is given as
F(x)=\argmax_{1\leq i\leq K}\Phi_{i}(x)
,
where
\Phi
is a neural network of the form

\Phi(x)=(f_{p}\circ f_{p-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1})(x).

Here
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{i-1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
with
d_{0}=d
and
d_{p}=K
.
We assume that each
f_{i}
belongs to a function class
\mathcal{F}_{i}\subset\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{i-1}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}})
.
We define a function class
\mathcal{F}
to which
\Phi
belongs
by

\mathcal{F}=\{\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K}:\Phi(x)=(f_{p}\circ\dots\circ f_{1})(x),\quad f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{i},\forall i\}.

For example, for
b>0
,

\mathcal{F}_{i}
is given as
\{h\mapsto W\phi(h):W\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i-1}\times d_{i}},\|W\|_{\mathrm{fro}}\leq b\}
if
i>1

and
\{h\mapsto Wh:W\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}\times d_{1}},\|W\|_{\mathrm{fro}}\leq b\}
if
i=1
,
where
\phi
is a link function (applied on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
entry-wise) with bounded operator norm
(i.e.,
\|\phi\|_{\mathrm{op}}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}\setminus\{0\}}\|\phi(x)\|_{2}/\|x\|_{2}<\infty
)
and
\|W\|_{\mathrm{fro}}
denotes the Frobenius norm of the matrix.
However, we do not assume the function class
\mathcal{F}_{i}
does not have this specific form.
We assume that each function class
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is a normed vector space with norm
\|\cdot\|
.
In the example above, we consider the operator norm, i.e., if
f(h)=\phi(Wh)
,
\|f\|
is defined
as
\|f\|_{\mathrm{op}}
.
Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be a finite i.i.d. sequence of samples drawn from
\mathcal{P}_{w}
.
We denote the corresponding empirical distribution by
\widehat{P}_{w}
, i.e.,
for a measurable function
f
on
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\widehat{P}_{w}}\left[f\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})
.

For
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{p})\in\prod_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
, we define the perturbed output

\Phi(x,\xi)
as
\Phi(x,\xi)=h_{p}(x,\xi)
, where

\displaystyle h_{1}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=f_{1}(x)+\xi_{1}\|x\|_{2},

\displaystyle h_{i}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=f_{i}(h_{i-1}(x,\xi))+\xi_{i}\|h_{i-1}(x,\xi)\|_{2},\quad\text{for }2\leq i\leq p.

Let
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
y\in[K]
.
We define
\Xi(\Phi,x,y)
by
\{\xi\in\prod_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}:\argmax_{i}\Phi_{i}(x,\xi)\neq y\}
.
Then, the all-layer margin
m(\Phi,x,y)
is defined as

m(\Phi,x,y)=\min_{\xi\in\Xi(\Phi,x,y)}\|\xi\|_{2},

where
\|\xi\|_{2}
is given by
\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{p}\|\xi_{i}\|^{2}_{2}}
.
Following
[
342
]
, we define
a variant of the all-layer margin that measures
robustness of
\Phi
with respect to input transformations defined by
\mathcal{B}(x)
as follows:

m_{\mathcal{B}}(\Phi,x):=\min_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(x)}m(F,x^{\prime},\argmax_{i}\Phi_{i}(x)).

Assumption
F.17

(c.f.
[
341
]
, Condition A.1)

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a normed space with norm
\|\cdot\|

and
\epsilon>0
.
We say
\mathcal{G}
satisfies the
\epsilon^{-2}
covering condition with complexity
\mathcal{C}_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathcal{G})
if
for all
\epsilon>0
, we have

\log\mathcal{N}_{\|\cdot\|}(\epsilon,\mathcal{G})\leq\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathcal{G})}{\epsilon^{2}}.

Here
\mathcal{N}_{\|\cdot\|}(\epsilon,\mathcal{G})
the
\epsilon
-covering number of
\mathcal{G}
.
We assume function class
\mathcal{F}_{i}
satisfies the
\epsilon^{-2}
covering condition with complexity
\mathcal{C}_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathcal{F}_{i})

for each
1\leq i\leq p
.

Throughout this section, we suppose that Assumption
F.17
holds.
Essentially, the following two propositions follows were proved by
Wei et al. 2020
:

Proposition F.18

(c.f.,
[
342
]
, Lemma D.6)
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
over the draw of the training data, for all
t\in(0,\infty)
,
any
\Phi\in\mathcal{F}
satisfies the following:

R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{P}_{w}}\left[\bm{1}(m_{\mathcal{B}}(\Phi,x)\leq t)\right]+\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{C}_{\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathcal{F}_{i})}{t\sqrt{n}}\right)+\zeta,

where
\zeta=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)+\log n}{n}}\right)
is a lower order term
and
F(x)=\argmax_{i\in[K]}\Phi_{i}(x)
.

Proposition F.19

(c.f.,
[
342
]
, Theorem D.3)
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
over the draw of the training data,
for all
t\in(0,\infty)
, any
\Phi\in\mathcal{F}
satisfies the following:

L_{w}(F,F_{\mathrm{pl}})=\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{P}_{w}}\left[\bm{1}(m(\Phi,x,F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x))\leq t)\right]+\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{C}_{\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathcal{F}_{i})}{t\sqrt{n}}\right)+\zeta,

where
\zeta=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)+\log n}{n}}\right)
is a lower order term
and
F(x)=\argmax_{i\in[K]}\Phi_{i}(x)
.

Remark F.20
.

Although we have proved Theorem
7.4
following
[
342
]
,
we had to provide our own proof due to some differences in theoretical assumptions
(e.g., in our case there does not necessarily exist the ground-truth classifier,
although they assumed the expansion property for each
P_{i}
,
we assume the expansion property for the weighted probability measure).
On the other hand, the proofs of
[
342
, Lemma D.6]

and
[
342
, Theorem D.3]
work for any distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}
and its empirical distribution

\widehat{P}
.
Since
\|w\|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\mathcal{S}^{c}_{\mathcal{B}}(F))=R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)
and

\|w\|_{1}\mathcal{P}_{w}(\{x:F(x)\neq F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\})=L_{w}(F,F_{\mathrm{pl}})
,
Proposition
F.18
and Proposition
F.19
follow from
the corresponding results in
[
342
]
.

Theorem F.21
.

Suppose Assumptions
7.2
,
7.3
, and
F.17
hold.
Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
over the draw of the training data,
for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in(0,\infty)
, and any neural network
\Phi
in
\mathcal{F}
,
we have the following:

\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F)=\frac{\gamma+1}{\gamma-1}\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{P}_{w}}\left[\bm{1}(m(\Phi,x,F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x))\leq t_{1})\right]+\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{P}_{w}}\left[\bm{1}(m_{\mathcal{B}}(\Phi,x)\leq t_{2})\right]\\
-\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})+2\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*})+\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F^{*})\\
+\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{C}_{\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathcal{F}_{i})}{t_{1}\sqrt{n}}\right)+\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{C}_{\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathcal{F}_{i})}{t_{2}\sqrt{n}}\right)+\zeta,

where
\zeta=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)+\log n}{n}}\right)
is a lower order term
and
F(x)=\argmax_{i\in[K]}\Phi_{i}(x)
.

Proof F.22
.

By (
17
) with
H=F^{*}
and

-\bm{1}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq F^{*}(x))\leq-\bm{1}(F_{\mathrm{pl}}(x)\neq j)+\bm{1}(F^{*}(x)\neq j)

for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
j\in[K]
, we obtain the following inequality:

\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F)\leq\frac{\gamma+1}{\gamma-1}L_{w}(F,F_{\mathrm{pl}})+\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F)+\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}R_{\mathcal{B},w}(F^{*})-\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F_{\mathrm{pl}})+2\mathrm{Err}_{w}(F^{*}).

Then, the statement of the theorem follows from Proposition
F.18
and Proposition
F.19
.

F
Proof of Proposition
7.1

Proof F.23
.

Let the average weighted consistency loss be
\mathcal{L}^{wt}_{u}=\frac{1}{|B|}\sum_{x\in B}\ell^{wt}_{u}(\hat{p}_{m}(x),p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x)),\mathbf{G})
this will be minimized if for each of
x\in B
the
\ell^{wt}_{u}(\hat{p}_{m}(x),p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x)),\mathbf{G})
is minimized. This expression can be expanded as:

\displaystyle\ell^{wt}_{u}(\hat{p}_{m}(x),p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x)),\mathbf{G})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))_{i}\log(p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))_{i})

\displaystyle=-C\sum_{i=1}^{K}\frac{(\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))_{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))_{j}}\log(p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))_{i})

\displaystyle=C\times\mathrm{H}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))\;||\;p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))).

Here we use H to denote the cross entropy between two distributions. As we don’t backpropogate gradients from the
\hat{p}_{m}(x)
(pseudo-label) branch of prediction network we can consider
C=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))_{j}
as a constant in our analysis. Also adding a constant term of entropy
\mathrm{H}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x)))
to cross entropy term and dropping constant
C
doesn’t change the outcome of minimization. Hence we have the following:

\displaystyle\min_{p_{m}}\mathrm{H}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))\;||\;p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x)))

\displaystyle=\min_{p_{m}}\mathrm{H}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))\;||\;p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x)))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathrm{H}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x)))

\displaystyle=\min_{p_{m}}\mathcal{D}_{KL}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))||p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))).

This final term is the
\mathcal{D}_{KL}(\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x)||p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))
which is obtained by using the identity
\mathcal{D}_{KL}(p,q)=\mathrm{H}(p,q)+\mathrm{H}(p)
where
p,q
are the two distributions.

F
Objective

F.1
Logit Adjusted Weighted Consistency Regularizer

As we have introduced weighted consistency regularizer in Eq. (
7.8
) for utilizing unlabeled data, we now provide logit adjusted variant of it for training deep networks in this section. We provide logit adjusted term for
\ell^{\mathrm{wt}}_{u}(\hat{p}_{m}(x),p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x),\mathbf{G})
below:

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{wt}}_{u}(\hat{p}_{m}(x),p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x),\mathbf{G})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{T}}\hat{p}_{m}(x))_{i}\log(p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))_{i})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathbf{G}^{\mathbf{T}}\hat{p}_{m}(x))_{i}\log\left(\frac{\exp({p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))_{i}})}{\sum_{j=1}^{K}\exp({p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))_{j}})}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathrm{\bm{D}}^{\mathbf{T}}\mathrm{\bm{M}}^{\mathbf{T}}\hat{p}_{m}(x))_{i}\log\left(\frac{\exp({p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))_{i}})}{\sum_{j=1}^{K}\exp({p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))_{j}})}\right)

The above expression comes from the decomposition
\mathbf{G}=\mathrm{MD}
. The above loss function can be converted into it’s logit adjusted equivalent variant by following transformation as suggested by
Narasimhan and Menon 2021
which is equivalent in terms of optimization of deep neural networks:

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{wt}}_{u}(\hat{p}_{m}(x),p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x),\mathbf{G})\equiv-\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\mathrm{\bm{M}}^{\mathbf{T}}\hat{p}_{m}(x))_{i}\log\left(\frac{\exp({p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))_{i}-\log(\bm{D}_{ii}}))}{\sum_{j=1}^{K}\exp({p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x))_{j}-\log(\bm{D}_{jj}}))}\right)

(21)

The above loss is the consistency loss
\ell^{\mathrm{wt}}_{u}
that we practically implement for
CSST
. Further in case
\hat{p}_{m}(x)
is a hard pseudo label
y
as in FixMatch, the above weighted consistency loss reduces to
\ell^{\mathrm{hyb}}(y,p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x)))
. Further in case the gain matrix
G
is diagonal the above loss will converge to
\ell^{\mathrm{LA}}(y,p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x)))
. Thus the weighted consistency regularizer can be converted to logit adjusted variants
\ell^{\mathrm{LA}}
and
\ell^{\mathrm{hyb}}
based on
\mathbf{G}
matrix.

F.2
CSST
(FixMatch)

In FixMatch, we use the prediction made by the model on a sample
x
after applying a weak augmentation
\alpha
and is used to get a hard pseudo label for the models prediction on a strongly augmented sample i.e.
\mathcal{A}(x)
. The set of weak augmentations include horizontal flip, We shall refer to this pseudo label as
\hat{p}_{m}(x)
. The list of strong augmentations are given in Table 12 of
Sohn et al. 2020
. Weak augmentations include padding, random horizontal flip and cropping to the desired dimensions (32X32 for CIFAR and 224X224 for ImageNet).
Given a batch of labeled and unlabeled samples
B_{s}
and
B_{u}
,
CSST
modifies the supervised and un-supervised component of the loss function depending upon the non-decomposable objective and its corresponding gain matrix
\mathbf{G}
at a given time during training. We assume that in the dataset, a sample
x
, be it labeled or unlabeled is already weakly augmented.
vanilla
FixMatch’s supervised componenet of the loss function is a simple cross entropy loss whereas in our
CSST
(FixMatch) it is replaced by
\ell_{s}^{\text{hyb}}
.

\mathcal{L}_{s}^{\text{hyb}}=\frac{1}{|B_{s}|}\sum_{x,y\in B_{s}}{\ell^{\text{hyb}}(y,s(x))}.

(22)

\mathcal{L}_{u}^{\mathrm{wt}}=\frac{1}{|B_{u}|}\sum_{x\in B_{u}}\mathds{1}_{(\mathcal{D}_{KL}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))\;||\;p_{m}(x))\leq\tau)}\ell^{\mathrm{wt}}_{u}(\hat{p}_{m}(x),p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x)),\mathbf{G})).

(23)

The component of the loss function for unlabeled data (i.e. consistency regularization) is where one of our contributions w.r.t the novel thresholding mechanism comes into light.
vanilla
FixMatch selects unlabeled samples for which consistency loss is non-zero, such that the model’s confidence on the most likely predicted class is above a certain threshold. We rather go for a threshold mechanism that select based on the basis of degree of distribution match to a target distribution based on
\mathbf{G}
. The final loss function
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{s}^{hyb}+\lambda_{u}\mathcal{L}_{u}^{\mathrm{wt}}
, i.e. a linear combination of
\mathcal{L}_{s}^{\mathrm{hyb}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{u}^{wt}
. Since for FixMatch we are dealing with Wide-ResNets and ResNets which are deep networks, as mentioned in Section
F.1
, we shall use the alternate logit adjusted formulation as mentioned in Eq. (
21
) as substitute for
\ell_{u}^{\mathrm{wt}}
in Eq. (
23
).

F.3
CSST
(UDA)

The loss function of UDA is a linear combination of supervised loss and consistency loss on unlabeled samples. The former is the cross entropy (CE) loss, while the latter for the unlabeled samples minimizes the KL-divergence between the model’s predicted label distribution on an input sample and its augmented sample. Often the predicted label distribution on the unaugmented sample is sharpened. The augmentation we used was a English-French-English backtranslation based on the MarianMT
[
130
]
fast neural machine translation model. In UDA supervised component of the loss is annealed using a method described as Training Signal Annealing (TSA), where the CE loss is considered only for those labeled samples whose
\max_{i}p_{m}(x)_{i}<\tau_{t}
, where
t
is a training time step. We observed that using TSA in a long tailed setting leads to overfitting on the head classes and hence chose to not include the same in our final implementation.

CSST
modifies the supervised and unsupervised component of the loss function in UDA depending upon a given objective and its corresponding gain matrix
\mathbf{G}
at a given time during training. The supervised component of the loss function for a given constrained optimization problem and a gain matrix
\mathbf{G}
, is the hybrid loss
\ell_{s}^{\text{hyb}}
. For the consistency regularizer part of the loss function, we minimize the KL-divergence between a target distribution and the model’s prediction label distribution on its augmented version.
The target distribution is
\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))
, where
\hat{p}_{m}(x)
is the sharpened prediction of the label distribution by the model. Given a batch of labeled and unlabeled samples
B_{s}
and
B_{u}
, the final loss function in
CSST
(UDA) is a linear combination of
\mathcal{L}_{s}^{hyb}
and
\mathcal{L}_{u}^{wt}
, i.e
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{s}^{hyb}+\lambda_{u}\mathcal{L}_{u}^{wt}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}^{\text{hyb}}=\frac{1}{|B_{s}|}\sum_{x,y\in B_{s}}{\ell^{\text{hyb}}(p_{m}(x),y)}.

(24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{u}^{wt}=\frac{1}{|B_{u}|}\sum_{x\in B_{u}}\mathds{1}_{(\mathcal{D}_{KL}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))\;||\;p_{m}(x))\leq\tau)}\ell^{\mathrm{wt}}_{u}(\hat{p}_{m}(x),p_{m}(\mathcal{A}(x),\mathbf{G})).

(25)

Since for UDA, we are dealing with DistilBERT, as mentioned in Section
F.1
, we shall use the alternate formulation as mentioned in Eq. (
21
) as substitute for
\ell_{u}^{\mathrm{wt}}
in Eq. above.

F
Threshold mechanism for diagonal Gain Matrix

Consider the case when the gain matrix is a diagonal matrix. The loss function
\mathcal{L}_{u}^{wt}(B_{u})
as defined in (
7.9
) makes uses of a threshold function that selects samples based on the KL divergence based threshold between the target distribution as defined by the gain matrix
\mathbf{G}
and the models predicted distribution of confidence over the classes.

\text{Threshold function }\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathds{1}_{(\mathcal{D}_{KL}(\left\lVert(\right\rVert\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))\;||\;p_{m}(x))\leq\tau)}

(26)

Since
\mathbf{G}
is a diagonal matrix and the pseudo-label
\hat{p}_{m}(x)
is one hot, the
\text{norm}(\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))
is a one-hot vector. The threshold function’s KL divergence based criterion can be expanded as follows where
\hat{y}
is the pseudo-label’s maximum class’s index:

\mathcal{D}_{KL}(\text{norm}(\mathbf{G}^{T}\hat{p}_{m}(x))||p_{m}(x))=-\log{p_{m}(x)}_{\hat{y}}<\tau\\

(27)

The above equations represents a threshold on the negative log-confidence of the model’s prediction for a given unlabeled sample, for the pseudo-label class (
\hat{y}
). This can be further simplified to
p_{m}(x)_{\hat{y}}\geq\exp{(-\tau)}
which is simply a threshold based on the model’s confidence. Since pseudo-label is generated from the model’s prediction, this threshold is nothing but a selection criterion to select only those samples whose maximum confidence for a predicted hard pseudo-label is above a fixed threshold. This is identical to the threshold function which is used in Fixmatch
[
297
]
i.e.
\max(p_{m}(x))\geq\exp(-\tau)
. In FixMatch this
\exp(-\tau)
is set to 0.95.

F
Dataset

CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100
[
167
]
.
are image classification datasets of images of size 32 X 32. Both the datasets have a size of 50k samples and by default, they have a uniform sample distribution among its classes. CIFAR-10 has 10 classes while CIFAR-100 has 100 classes. The test set is a balanced set of 10k images.

ImageNet-100
[
267
]
.
is an image classification dataset carved out of ImageNet-1k by selecting the first 100 classes. The distribution of samples is uniform with 1.3k samples per class. The test set contains 50 images per class. All have a resolution of 224X224, the same as the original ImageNet-1k dataset.

IMDb
[
206
]
.
dataset is a binary text sentiment classification dataset. The data distribution is uniform by default and has a total 25k samples in both trainset and testset. In this work, we converted the dataset into a longtailed version of
\rho=10,100
and selected 1k labeled samples while truncating the labels of the rest and using them as unlabeled samples.

DBpedia-14
[
182
]
.
is a topic classification dataset with a uniform distribution of labeled samples. The dataset has 14 classes and has a total of 560k samples in the trainset and 70k samples in the test set. Each sample, apart from the content, also has title of the article that could be used for the task of topic classification. In our experiments, we only make use of the content.

F
Algorithms

We provide a detailed description of algorithms used for optimizing non decomposable objectives through
CSST
(FixMatch) ans
CSST
(UDA). Algorithm
4
is used for experiments in Section
7.5
for maximizing worst-case recall (i.e. min recall using
CSST
(FixMatch) and
CSST
(UDA)). Algorithm
5
is used for experiments in Section
7.5
for maximizing recall under coverage constraints (i.e. min coverage experiments on CIFAR10-LT, CIFAR100-LT and ImageNet100-LT).

Algorithm 4

CSST
-based Algorithm for Maximizing Worst-case Recall

Inputs: Training set
S_{s}
(labeled) and
S_{u}
(unlabeled) , Validation set
S^{\text{val}}
, Step-size
\omega\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, Class priors
\pi

Initialize: Classifier
F^{0}
, Multipliers
\bm{\lambda}^{0}\in\Delta_{K-1}

for

t=0
to
T-1

do

Update
\bm{\lambda}
:

\lambda^{t+1}_{i}=\lambda^{t}_{i}\exp\left(-\omega\cdot\text{recall}_{i}[F^{t}]\right),\forall i,

\bm{\lambda}=\left\lVert(\right\rVert\bm{\lambda})

\mathbf{G}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda^{t+1}_{1}/\pi_{1},\ldots,\lambda^{t+1}_{K}/\pi_{K})

Compute
\ell_{u}^{\text{wt}}
,
\ell_{s}^{\text{hyb}}
using
\mathbf{G}

Cost-sensitive Learning (CSL) for FixMatch:

B_{u}\sim S_{u},B_{s}\sim S_{s}
// Sample batches of data

F^{t+1}\,\in\,\arg\min_{F}\sum_{B_{u},B_{s}}\lambda_{u}\mathcal{L}_{u}^{\mathrm{wt}}+\mathcal{L}_{s}^{\mathrm{hyb}}
// Replaced by few steps of SGD

end

for

return

F^{T}

Algorithm 5

CSST
-based Algorithm for Maximizing Mean Recall s.t. per class coverage ¿ 0.95/K

Inputs: Training set
S_{s}
(labeled) and
S_{u}
(unlabeled) , Validation set
S^{\text{val}}
, Step-size
\omega\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, Class priors
\pi

Initialize: Classifier
F^{0}
, Multipliers
\bm{\lambda}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}

for

t=0
to
T-1

do

Update
\bm{\lambda}
:

\lambda^{t+1}_{i}=\lambda^{t}_{i}-\omega\big(\text{cov}_{i}[F^{t}]-\frac{0.95}{K}\big),\forall i

\lambda^{t+1}_{i}\,=\,\max\{0,\lambda^{t+1}_{i}\},\forall i\in[K]
// Projection to
\mathbb{R}_{+}

\mathbf{G}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda^{t+1}_{1}/\pi_{1},\ldots,\lambda^{t+1}_{K}/\pi_{K})+\bf{1}_{K}\bm{\lambda}^{\top}

Compute
\ell_{u}^{\text{wt}}
,
\ell_{s}^{\text{hyb}}
using
\mathbf{G}

Cost-sensitive Learning (CSL) for FixMatch:

B_{u}\sim S_{u},B_{s}\sim S_{s}
// Sample batches of data

F^{t+1}\,\in\,\arg\min_{F}\sum_{B_{u},B_{s}}\lambda_{u}\mathcal{L}_{u}^{\mathrm{wt}}+\mathcal{L}_{s}^{\mathrm{hyb}}
// Replaced by few steps of SGD

end

for

return

F
Details of Experiments and Hyper-parameters

The experiment of
\max_{F}\min_{i}\text{recall}_{i}[f]
and
\max_{F}\text{recall}[F]\text{ s.t. }\text{cov}_{i}[F]>\frac{0.95}{K},\forall i\in[K]
was performed on the long tailed version of CIFAR-10, IMDb(
\rho=10,100
) and DBpedia-14 datasets. This was because the optimization of the aforementioned 2 objectives is stable for cases with low number of classes. Hence the objective of
\max_{F}\min(\text{recall}_{\mathcal{H}}[F],\text{ recall}_{\mathcal{T}}[F])
and

\max_{F}\text{recall}[F]\text{ s.t. }\min_{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{T}}\text{cov}_{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{T}}[F]>\frac{0.95}{K}
is a relatively easier objective for datasets with large number of classes, hence were the optimization objectives for CIFAR-100 and ImageNet-100 long tailed datasets. For all experiments for a given dataset, we used the same values for a given common hyperparameter. We ablated the threshold for our novel unlabeled sample selection criterion(
\tau
) and the ratio of labeled and unlabeled samples, given fixed number of unlabeled samples(
\mu
) and are available in Fig.
4b
.

All experiments were done on a variety of GPUs, with primarily Nvidia A5000 (24GB) with occasional use of Nvidia A100 (80GB) and Nvidia RTX3090 (24GB). For finetuning DistilBERT and all experiments with ImageNet-100 dataset we used PyTorch data parallel over 4 A5000s. Training was done till no significant change in metrics was observed. The detailed list of computation used per experiment type and dataset have been tabulated in Table
F.1
and Table

.

F
Statistical Analysis

We establish the statistical soundness and validity of our results we ran our experiments on 3 different seeds. Due to the computational requirements for some of the experiments (
\approx
2days) we chose to run the experiments on multiple seeds for a subset of tasks i.e. for maximising the minimum recall among all classes for CIFAR-10 LT. We observe that the std. deviation is significantly smaller than the average values for mean recall and min. recall and our performance metrics fall within our std. deviation hence validating the stability and soundness of training.

F
Additional Details

F.1
Formal Statement Omitted in Sec.
7.2.2

In Sec.
7.2.2
,
we stated that learning with the hybrid loss
\ell^{\mathrm{hyb}}
gives the Bayes-optimal classifier
for the CSL (
7.2
).
However, due to space constraint, we did not provide a formal statement.
In this section, we provide a formal statement of it for clarity.

Proposition F.24

(
[
222
]
Proposition 4)
.

For any diagonal matrix
\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
with
D_{ii}>0,\forall i
,

\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
,
and
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{D}
, the hybrid loss
\ell^{\mathrm{hyb}}
is calibrated for
\mathbf{G}
.
That is, for any model-prediction
\widehat{p}_{m}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K}
that minimizes

\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim D}\left[\ell^{\mathrm{hyb}}(y,\widehat{p}_{m}(x))\right]
, the associated classifier
F(x)=\argmax_{y\in[K]}\left(\widehat{p}_{m}\right)_{i}(x)
is the Bayes-optimal classifier for CSL (
7.2
).

F.2
Comparison with the
(a,\widetilde{c})
-expansion Property in
[
342
]

We compare the
c
-expansion property with
(a,\widetilde{c})
-expansion property proposed by
[
342
]
,
where
a\in(0,1)
and
\widetilde{c}>1
.
Here we say a distribution
Q
on
\mathcal{X}
satisfies the
(a,\widetilde{c})
-expansion property
if
Q(\mathcal{N}(S))\geq\widetilde{c}
for any
S\subset\mathcal{X}
with
Q(S)\leq a
.
If
Q
satisfies
(a,\widetilde{c})
-expansion property
[
342
]

with
\widetilde{c}>1
, then

Q
satisfies the
c
-expansion property, where
the function
c
is defined as follows.

c(p)=\widetilde{c}
if
p\leq a
and
c(p)=1
otherwise.
On the other hand, if
Q
-satisfies
c
-expansion property,
then for any
a\in(0,1)
and
S\subseteq\mathcal{X}
with
Q(S)\leq a
,
we have
Q(\mathcal{N}(S))\geq c(Q(S))Q(S)\geq c(a)Q(S)
since
c
is non-increasing.
Therefore,
Q
satisfies the
(a,c(a))
-expansion property.
Thus, we could say these two conditions are equivalent.
To simplify our analysis, we use our definition of the expansion property.

In addition,
Wei et al. 2020
showed that the
(a,\widetilde{c})
-expansion property is realistic for vision. Although they assumed the
(a,c)
-expansion property for each
P_{i}

(1\leq i\leq K)
and we assume the
c
-expansion property for
\mathcal{P}_{w}
, it follows that the
c
-expansion property for
\mathcal{P}_{w}
is also realistic for vision, since
\mathcal{P}_{w}
is a linear combination of
P_{i}
.

In this section, we compare
CSST
(FixMatch) against contemporary semi-supervised learning techniques namely CReST
[
339
]
, DARP
[
151
]
,
vanilla
(FixMatch)
[
297
]
and FlexMatch
[
376
]
. We compared these methods on the long-tailed CIFAR-10 (
\rho
= 100) and CIFAR-100 (
\rho
= 10) datasets. The objective for long-tailed CIFAR-10 dataset was to maximise the worst-case recall (
7.2.1
) and average recall, subject to a per-class coverage constraint (
7.4
). For CIFAR-100 LT dataset, we compare these methods for the objectives maximizing HT recall (
7.10
) and recall under HT coverage constraints(
7.11
). For the objectives (
7.2.1
) and (
7.10
), DARP achieves the best average recall yet it suffers on the worst-case recall.
CSST
(FixMatch) outperforms it on CIFAR-100 and the has superior worst-case recall on CIFAR-10. A similar observation can be made for the objectives (
7.4
) and (
7.11
) where it is only
CSST
(FixMatch) that has the highest min. coverage and either has superior mean recall or has negligible decrease in the avg. recall.
